[]{#c31.xhtml}

::: {#c31.xhtml#book-content}
::: {#c31.xhtml#sbo-rt-content}
<div>

# []{#c31.xhtml#Page_209 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="209"}[]{#c31.xhtml#c31}[31]{.chapterNumber} [The LinkedIn Profile Makeover]{.chapterTitle} {#c31.xhtml#c31_1}

</div>

::: {.section aria-label="chapter opening"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0001}

*Your LinkedIn profile is different from your résumé. It needs to sell
who you are, what you know, and how you help---not where you\'ve been.
It must speak directly to the problems your prospects are trying to
solve right now. It must trigger curiosity, invite conversation,
demonstrate authority, and position you as a trusted expert*.

Your LinkedIn profile is your digital storefront. And like any good
storefront, it needs to be designed with your customer in mind. When
your profile makes your value obvious, when it gives people a reason to
pause, read, and learn, you open the door to more conversations,
credibility, and opportunities.

You don\'t need to be a LinkedIn guru or hire a copywriter to make your
profile pop. You just need to follow a simple, proven process that helps
you clarify your message and tell the right story to the right people.

[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_210 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="210"}Over the course of the next few chapters we\'ll walk
you through the process of transforming your LinkedIn profile from a
digital business card into a dynamic asset that becomes the core
foundation of your personal brand, and becomes an inbound lead machine.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-143"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0002}

## Grabbing Attention Above the Fold {#c31.xhtml#head-2-143}

Before anyone scrolls through your LinkedIn profile, they\'ve already
made a snap judgment. If it doesn\'t grab attention in those first
crucial seconds, you lose them.

That\'s the power---and the danger---of what\'s known as the "above the
fold" section of your LinkedIn profile. It\'s the first thing a visitor
sees when they land on your page. Your name, headline, photo, background
image, and contact details are all right there, front and center.

Every element of the above the fold section of your LinkedIn profile
needs to work together to tell people exactly who you are, what you do,
and why they should care.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-144"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0003}

## Step 1: Save Your Profile Before Editing {#c31.xhtml#head-2-144}

Since LinkedIn lacks an "undo" option, before you make any
modifications, download your profile as a PDF:

1.  [Go to your LinkedIn profile.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0001}
2.  [Click on "More" (under your profile image) and choose "Save to
    PDF."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0002}
3.  [Keep it in an easily accessible place for future
    reference.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0003}

This acts as a safeguard in case you want to reinstate earlier content
and is a benchmark reference as you make changes.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-145"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0004}

## Step 2: Cover Image Banner {#c31.xhtml#head-2-145}

It all starts with your *cover image*. This is prime digital real
estate---your personal billboard. Your banner should tell a story
[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_211 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="211"}the moment someone lands on your profile. Do not leave
it blank or use generic stock images.

Think of it as a visual handshake. It should reflect your brand, your
company, and your value. Incorporate your organization\'s colors,
messaging, and personality.

Highlight upcoming events, webinars, or key announcements. Switch it out
based on what you\'re promoting or where you\'re showing up.

LinkedIn Premium allows users to upload up to five rotating
banners---like a mini slideshow. Use that feature to spotlight your
expertise, reinforce brand messaging, and keep your profile dynamic and
engaging.

### Upload Your Cover Image to LinkedIn

1.  [Go to your profile.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0004}
2.  [If you\'re doing this for the first time, click on the camera icon
    in the top‐right of the cover image area. To replace an existing
    banner, click on the pencil icon in the top‐right corner of the
    cover image area.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0005}
3.  [Click "Upload Photo" or "Create a Slideshow" (for premium
    members).]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0006}
4.  [Select the banner file you saved on your
    computer.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0007}
5.  [Crop the photo, use photo filters, adjust, change the position and
    size, or rotate your cover image as needed, then click
    "Apply."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0008}
6.  [View your profile and continue to adjust until you have it dialed
    in.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0009}

::: section
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0005}

### Design Your Banner in Canva {#c31.xhtml#head-3-93}

In our opinion, Canva is hands down the best tool for building your
cover image banner. Every sales pro should have an account and know how
to use it.[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_212 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="212"}

1.  [Set up a free account on
    [Canva.com](http://Canva.com).]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0010}
2.  [To use a pre‐sized template, visit
    <https://www.canva.com/create/linkedin-banners/>.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0011}
3.  [Build your cover image banner.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0012}

If you create your cover image on a different platform, follow these
image specifications:

-   [Use a JPG or PNG file type.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0013}
-   [Keep the file size under 8MB.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0014}
-   [Dimensions should be 1584 × 396 pixels.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0015}
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-146"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0006}

## Step 3: Headshot {#c31.xhtml#head-2-146}

Your headshot is the first visual cue people get about you---and it
matters more than most salespeople think. According to
[PhotoFeeler.com](http://PhotoFeeler.com), "Profile photos are so
essential to modern communication that a good one becomes a basic
necessity. And that couldn\'t be truer than for those of us whose
professional lives are tied to social media profiles."

This isn\'t the place for a selfie, an AI avatar, a cropped wedding
photo, or your favorite tailgate memory. No cats, no kids, no beer
bottles, and definitely no mirror pics from your gym. A professional
headshot---one that looks like the person who will show up to the
meeting---is nonnegotiable.

It should be taken in good lighting, at a flattering angle, and on a
clean, neutral background. Drop the cheesy poses: no crossed arms, chin
grabs, or staring wistfully into the horizon. You\'re not auditioning
for a cologne ad. These poses make you come off like a self‐centered
schmuck.

Instead, look directly into the camera. Be open. Be approachable. And
smile---genuinely. PhotoFeeler analyzed over 60,000 ratings and found
that a real, confident smile significantly boosts perceived competence,
likability, and influence. []{#c31.xhtml#Page_213 .pagebreak
role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="213"}Few things create a positive
impression faster than a warm, sincere smile.

If you\'ve updated your look---shaved a beard, changed your hairstyle,
switched up your glasses---update your photo. The goal is for your
prospects to instantly recognize you when your meeting starts. That
recognition, even subconsciously, builds comfort. And comfort builds
connection.

### Upload Your Headshot to LinkedIn

1.  [Go to your profile.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0016}
2.  [Click your existing photo (or the photo placeholder if you don\'t
    have one yet).]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0017}
3.  [Click "add photo."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0018}
4.  [Upload your new photo.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0019}
5.  [Crop, edit, and adjust as needed: using the cropping tool to center
    your face and the built‐in zoom, rotate, and filters, as
    needed.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0020}
6.  [Set your photo to public for visibility and branding (unless there
    is a security reason that you need to make your photo
    private).]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0021}
7.  [Once you\'re happy with the image and settings, click "Save
    photo."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0022}

For best results, use the same headshot across all your social
platforms: LinkedIn, X (formerly Twitter), Instagram, Facebook, Slack,
Zoom---you name it. Your image is like your logo. Repetition builds
familiarity. Familiarity builds trust. And trust opens doors.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-147"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0007}

## Step 4: Headline {#c31.xhtml#head-2-147}

A few months back, right before the start of a LinkedIn Edge workshop
for commercial bankers, a veteran banker named Eric pulled aside
Jack---our instructor---with a skeptical tone.

[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_214 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="214"}"Are you the one running this class?" he asked. Jack
nodded. "My president forced me to be here," Eric grumbled. The message
was clear: I\'m not buying what you\'re selling.

Later, when the group reviewed profile headlines, Jack noticed that
Eric\'s simply read: "Vice President, Commercial Lender."

"How do you feel about it?" Jack asked.

"I love it," Eric said with a hint of arrogance.

Jack didn\'t flinch. "There are around 100,000 people in the US with
that same title. If you want to blend in, that\'s fine. But is that
really what you want?"

Eric paused. "No. I want to stand out. I want to be seen as the banker
who gets it done for my clients."

"Perfect," Jack said. "Let\'s try something different. How about: 'The
Banker in Charge of Getting Things Done in Hartford'?"

Eric laughed. "That sounds ridiculous. But fine---I\'ll give it 30
days."

Thirty days later, Eric emailed Jack in disbelief. "You won\'t believe
this. I had three people reach out to talk about banking. One CFO even
said he\'d been looking his whole career for a banker who gets things
done."

Your LinkedIn headline is prime real estate. At just 220 characters,
it\'s one of the most valuable pieces of digital property you own. And
yet most sales professionals squander it by listing nothing more than
their job title and company name.

"Sales Manager at XYZ Corp" won\'t cut it. That headline puts you in the
pile with every other sales manager out there. It doesn\'t speak to who
you help, how you help them, or why anyone should care.

Your headline is your positioning statement. Think of it as your
elevator pitch in a sentence---a clear, concise signal that
[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_215 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="215"}grabs attention and says, "This is who I help, and
here\'s how I do it."

When your headline is written right, it sparks curiosity, establishes
credibility, and starts conversations. And when people are scrolling
LinkedIn, your headline---along with your photo and name---is what
determines whether they click or keep scrolling.

::: section
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0008}

### The Headline Formula: Who You Help + Outcomes You Deliver + How You Help {#c31.xhtml#head-3-94}

Use this simple framework to guide your headline: I help \[Target
Audience\] \[Achieve Outcomes\] by \[What You Do\].

Here are some examples:

-   [Helping CFOs in SaaS Startups Reduce Burn and Scale Confidently
    with Fractional Finance Leadership.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0023}
-   [I Help Sales Teams Shorten the Sales Cycle and Close More Deals
    Through Practical Training and Coaching.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0024}
-   [Equipping Construction Leaders with the Tools to Hire Smarter and
    Build Faster.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0025}
-   [Helping B2B Sales Teams Master Prospecting and Close More Deals
    Through Practical, No‐Fluff Sales Training.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0026}
-   [Empowering IT Leaders to Reduce Downtime and Boost Productivity
    Through AI‐Powered Network Solutions.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0027}
-   [Helping CMOs Win Budget Battles with Metrics‐Driven Campaigns That
    Deliver Real Results.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0028}
-   [Helping Startup Founders Sleep at Night by Building Financial
    Models, Forecasts, and Systems that Scale.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0029}

Avoid buzzwords. Focus on clarity, specificity, and outcomes.

```{=html}
<aside>
```
::: {.top .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::

::: {.section .feature3}
### AI PRO TIP

Headline Prompt: I\'m updating my LinkedIn headline. I\'m a \[job
title\] who works with \[target audience\]. I help them \[solve specific
problem or achieve result\]. Give me five options for a LinkedIn
headline under 220 characters that will position me as a trusted expert
and attract ideal prospects. Please use the framework: Who You Help +
Outcomes You Deliver + How You Help.

::: {.bottom .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::
:::

```{=html}
</aside>
```
:::

[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_216 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="216"}

::: section
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0010}

### How to Update Your LinkedIn Headline {#c31.xhtml#head-3-95}

Done right, your headline becomes a conversation starter, a credibility
builder, and a magnet for the right kind of attention. Take these steps
to get started:

1.  [Go to your profile.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0030}
2.  [Click the pencil icon near your name and
    headline.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0031}
3.  [In the Headline field, delete your current text and add your new
    positioning statement. (Keep it under 220 characters---including
    spaces.)]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0032}
4.  [Click "Save."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0033}

Revisit your headline every 6--12 months to make sure it still reflects
who you help, how you help, and the value you deliver today---not who
you were two jobs ago.
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-148"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0011}

## Step 5: Name Pronunciation and Elevator Pitch {#c31.xhtml#head-2-148}

LinkedIn\'s Name Pronunciation feature is about more than phonetics.
It\'s an underrated opportunity to stand out.

In 10 seconds, you can instantly make your profile warmer, more human,
and more memorable. When used strategically, []{#c31.xhtml#Page_217
.pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="217"}this feature becomes a
mini elevator pitch---spoken in your own voice---that reinforces who you
help and the value you bring. Use it to:

-   [Clearly pronounce your name so there\'s no
    guesswork.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0034}
-   [Offer a short, human‐centered positioning
    statement.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0035}

Here\'s an example: "Hi, I\'m Brynne Tillman. I help B2B sales teams use
LinkedIn to start better conversations without sounding salesy."

::: section
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0012}

### How to Record Your Name Pronunciation {#c31.xhtml#head-3-96}

You can only add this feature using the LinkedIn mobile app.

1.  [Open the LinkedIn app on your phone.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0036}
2.  [Tap your profile photo in the top left, then tap "View
    Profile."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0037}
3.  [Tap the pencil icon next to your profile
    photo.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0038}
4.  [Scroll down to "Name Pronunciation" and tap the microphone to
    record.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0039}
5.  [Speak clearly and slowly---you only get 10
    seconds.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0040}
6.  [Save the recording and preview it to make sure it sounds
    right.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0041}

Practice your pitch a few times before recording. Be natural. Smile.
Speak like you would in a first meeting. That\'s what makes it stick.

This little audio clip adds warmth and personality to your digital
presence and creates an audio brand and voiceprint that prospects
remember.
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-149"}
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0013}

## Step 6: Make It Easy to Contact You {#c31.xhtml#head-2-149}

When a prospect is ready to take the next step, make it easy for them to
reach you. Nothing stalls inbound lead momentum faster than a missing
phone number or email.[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_218 .pagebreak
role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="218"}

-   [Start with your email. Use a professional address that builds
    trust. That means <yourname@company.com>, not
    <salesguy1978@gmail.com>.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0042}
-   [Add links to your website, calendar, lead magnet, blog, or podcast.
    This is where you point people who are interested in learning more
    or booking time with you. Treat it like your call‐to‐action
    space.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0043}
-   [Finally, add your phone number. Some sales professionals hesitate
    to include it. Don\'t. You are in sales. Your phone ringing is the
    goal.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0044}
-   [If you\'re concerned about privacy, use a VoIP number like Google
    Voice or OpenPhone. That way, you stay accessible without handing
    out your personal number.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0045}
-   [Add your birthday month and day. Each year, when people drop in to
    say happy birthday, use that moment to reconnect and deepen the
    relationship. It\'s one of the easiest and most organic ways to gain
    visibility.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0046}

::: section
[]{#c31.xhtml#c31-sec-0014}

### How to Update Your LinkedIn Contact Information {#c31.xhtml#head-3-97}

The bottom line is that your contact info needs to be complete, current,
and visible to your connections. Buyers shouldn\'t have to dig. When
they\'re ready, you need to be ready.

1.  [Go to your profile.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0047}
2.  [At the top‐right corner, next to your photo and headline, click the
    pencil icon to open the profile editor.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0048}
3.  [In the profile editor, scroll until you see the contact info
    section. Click the pencil icon next to it.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0049}
4.  [Add your professional email (<yourname@company.com>) and set it as
    your primary. You can associate multiple emails with your LinkedIn
    account, but your primary email is what connections
    see.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0050}
5.  [[]{#c31.xhtml#Page_219 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="219"}Enter your business mobile, office, or VoIP number.
    Be sure to select the correct country code.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0051}
6.  [You can add multiple links under "Website." Use descriptive labels
    like "Company Website," "Schedule a Call," or "Download My
    Guide."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0052}
7.  [Add your birthday. LinkedIn will notify your connections on your
    special day and create an opportunity for
    reengagement.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0053}
8.  [Once you\'ve updated your information, click
    "Save."]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0054}
9.  [You can also adjust who can see each piece of contact information.
    Set visibility to "Your connections" for professional access, or
    "Only you" if you\'re storing info for your own
    records.]{#c31.xhtml#c31-li-0055}

Remember, if people can't easily contact you they may just move on.
:::
:::
:::
:::

[]{#c32.xhtml}

::: {#c32.xhtml#book-content}
::: {#c32.xhtml#sbo-rt-content}
<div>

# []{#c32.xhtml#Page_220 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="220"}[]{#c32.xhtml#c32}[32]{.chapterNumber} [The Middle Hooks]{.chapterTitle} {#c32.xhtml#c32_1}

</div>

::: {.section aria-label="chapter opening"}
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0001}

*Once you\'ve grabbed your profile visitor\'s attention with the
above‐the‐fold elements, the real work begins. Now you\'ve got to hook
them. To make them stop scrolling and think, "This person gets it. This
person understands me."*

The middle section of your LinkedIn profile is where interest turns into
intent. It\'s the meat of your profile---the substance behind the
sizzle. It\'s where you back up your headline and prove that you\'re
more than just a clever tagline.

This is your LinkedIn profile lead‐generation engine. Here\'s where the
real story gets told---who you help, how you help, why it matters, and
what makes you different. The middle of your profile includes four core
sections:

-   [**About:** This is your story. It\'s where you connect the dots
    between your audience\'s challenges and the outcomes you deliver.
    Done right, it positions you as a guide they can
    trust.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0001}
-   [[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_221 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="221"}**Featured:** Your proof zone. Showcase content,
    wins, or resources that build authority and back up your brand
    promise. This is also a place where you can generate inbound
    leads.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0002}
-   [**Services:** A short list of what you actually do. This makes it
    easy for people to quickly understand how they can work with
    you.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0003}
-   [**Activity:** This section showcases your LinkedIn posts, comments,
    videos, images, articles, and documents.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0004}

The good news for you is that most LinkedIn profile middles are dull,
self‐centered, and completely forgettable, which means that when you do
it right, you own the room.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-150"}
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0002}

## Step 7: Tell Your Story in the About Section {#c32.xhtml#head-2-150}

Most LinkedIn About sections are a bland wall of text packed with career
highlights, job titles, and word‐salad jargon no buyer cares about.
It\'s a résumé in disguise. It\'s boring. And that\'s exactly why no one
reads it.

Your prospects aren\'t scrolling LinkedIn looking for someone with "20
years of experience" or a string of awards. They\'re looking for someone
who can solve a problem. Period.

This isn\'t the place to prove you\'re qualified. It\'s the place to
prove you understand the challenges, pain, and problems faced by your
target ICP prospects and stakeholders.

The About section is your hook. It\'s your chance to grab your buyer by
their heart and say, "I hear you. I get it. I\'ve seen this before. And
I can help."

When you speak their language---when you show them that you get their
pain and that you\'ve got the answers---you become someone worth talking
to. If your About section isn\'t doing this, it\'s costing you leads.

[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_222 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="222"}

::: section
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0003}

### Leverage the PIPA Framework to Craft a Scroll‐Stopping About Section {#c32.xhtml#head-3-98}

The best About sections meet your prospect where they are---inside their
problems---and walk them straight to a solution that you are uniquely
positioned to provide.

The *PIPA framework* gives you a simple structure for crafting powerful,
relevant messaging that hooks attention, builds authority, and compels
action.

::: section
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0004}

#### *P = Pain* {#c32.xhtml#head-4-1}

Start by naming the core problem your ideal customer is facing. Be
specific. This is how you signal, "I get you." For example:

> *Most founders hit a wall when it\'s time to scale. The books are a
> mess. Cash flow\'s unpredictable. Financial decisions are based on gut
> feel---not data. And the stress? It\'s constant*.

Enter the conversation that is already happening in your prospect\'s
mind. The more clearly you articulate their pain, the more they\'ll
believe you can solve it. The key is stepping into their shoes and
leveraging empathy to view the situation through their lens.
:::

::: section
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0005}

#### *I = Insight* {#c32.xhtml#head-4-2}

Next, share a sharp, experience‐backed insight that reframes the
problem---or shows your prospect a better path. This is where you earn
credibility by showing you understand how to solve their problem at a
deeper level. For example:

> *You don\'t need more spreadsheets---you need clarity. Real visibility
> into what\'s working, what\'s broken, and where your business is
> bleeding cash. The right financial strategy makes decisions easier,
> faster, and way more profitable*.

[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_223 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="223"}Insight proves you\'re not just another vendor. You\'re
someone who knows how to solve problems.
:::

::: section
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0006}

#### *P = Position* {#c32.xhtml#head-4-3}

Now position yourself as uniquely qualified to help them solve the
problem you just described. Keep it crisp, human, and focused on
outcomes. For example:

> *I help founders of high‐growth startups take control of their
> finances so that they can scale faster, while assuring investors that
> they are in control, with fractional CFO support that\'s hands‐on,
> founder‐friendly, and built for speed*.

This isn\'t a product pitch. It\'s a positioning statement. It answers
the question *Why should I trust you to solve this for me?*
:::

::: section
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0007}

#### *A = Action* {#c32.xhtml#head-4-4}

Finish strong with a simple, clear call to action. Don\'t beg. Don\'t
pitch. Just open the door.

> *If you\'re navigating growth and the numbers are starting to feel
> like quicksand, let\'s talk. Whether or not we work together, I\'ll
> share some best practices that will help you make sense of the numbers
> and gain peace of mind*. ![](images/c32g001.png){role="presentation"
> width="51" height="29"} \[Email\] \|
> ![](images/c32g002.png){role="presentation" width="13" height="35"}
> \[Phone Number\] \| ![](images/c32g003.png){role="presentation"
> width="48" height="44"} \[Calendar Link\]

### How to edit your LinkedIn About section {#c32.xhtml#head-3-99}

1.  [Go to your profile.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0005}
2.  [Scroll to the "About" section. If you don\'t have one yet, LinkedIn
    will prompt you to "Add About." If it\'s already there, you\'ll see
    a pencil icon in the upper‐right corner of the section. This opens
    the editor, where you can write or update your About
    section.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0006}
3.  [[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_224 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="224"}Write or paste your copy.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0007}
4.  [Check the formatting. Use short paragraphs, white space, and
    occasional line breaks for readability. Bullet points or bold text
    won\'t format in this section, so structure
    matters.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0008}
5.  [Click "Save." Once you\'re happy with it, click the blue Save
    button. Your About section is now live.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0009}
6.  [Review on mobile. View your profile on your phone to make sure the
    formatting looks clean. Most people will check you out on mobile
    first.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0010}
:::
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-151"}
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0008}

## About Section Examples and AI Prompts {#c32.xhtml#head-2-151}

Here you\'ll find examples of effective About statements and an AI
prompt framework to help you get started elevating yours.

### Example 1: Business Development Rep/Fleet Solutions Consultant {.listhead}

-   [**Pain:** Managing a fleet is expensive, time‐consuming, and full
    of hidden risks. Maintenance delays. Compliance headaches.
    Unexpected breakdowns. It all slows you down and drains your bottom
    line.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0011}
-   [**Insight:** Most companies think they\'re saving money by owning
    trucks. But when you add up the real costs---capital, downtime, lost
    productivity, new technology, emissions compliance---leasing starts
    to look like the smarter move.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0012}
-   [**Position:** I help fleet managers, logistics teams, and
    operations leaders move from chaos to control with full‐service
    truck leasing and maintenance solutions.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0013}
-   [**Action:** If you\'re exploring ways to improve uptime, reduce
    costs, or scale faster, let\'s talk. I\'ll help you break it down so
    you can make a smarter decision.
    ![](images/c32g001.png){role="presentation" width="51" height="29"}
    \[Email\] \| ![](images/c32g002.png){role="presentation" width="13"
    height="35"} \[Phone Number\] \|
    ![](images/c32g003.png){role="presentation" width="48" height="44"}
    \[Calendar Link\]]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0014}

### Example 2: Construction Equipment, Territory Sales Representative {.listhead}

-   [[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_225 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="225"}**Pain:** Downtime eats profits. And when equipment
    fails, delays follow. Projects fall behind, crews get frustrated,
    and revenue slips through your fingers.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0015}
-   [**Insight:** The right machine isn\'t just a tool---it\'s a partner
    in productivity. And a good partner should keep you running,
    competitive, and profitable.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0016}
-   [**Position:** I work with contractors and construction leaders to
    equip their teams with machines that deliver performance,
    reliability, and ROI---backed by the service and support to keep
    jobs on track.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0017}
-   [**Action:** If you\'re planning a purchase, lease or reviewing your
    fleet strategy, I\'d be happy to walk you through options that fit
    your work and your margins.
    ![](images/c32g001.png){role="presentation" width="51" height="29"}
    \[Email\] \| ![](images/c32g002.png){role="presentation" width="13"
    height="35"} \[Phone Number\] \|
    ![](images/c32g003.png){role="presentation" width="48" height="44"}
    \[Calendar Link\]]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0018}

### Example 3: IT Consultant for Healthcare Systems {.listhead}

-   [**Pain:** Many healthcare IT systems are bloated, slow, and
    stitched together with legacy software. Providers spend more time
    fighting the tools than serving patients.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0019}
-   [**Insight:** Technology should enable better care---not block it.
    Smart integration, streamlined workflows, and modern platforms
    unlock speed and precision where it matters
    most.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0020}
-   [**Position:** I work with healthcare organizations to modernize
    their IT infrastructure---cutting complexity, reducing risk, and
    freeing up teams to focus on care.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0021}
-   [**Action:** If your systems are slowing you down, let\'s talk. A
    20‐minute strategy call could uncover weeks\' worth of operational
    gains. ![](images/c32g001.png){role="presentation" width="51"
    height="29"} \[Email\] \|
    ![](images/c32g002.png){role="presentation" width="13" height="35"}
    \[Phone Number\] \| ![](images/c32g003.png){role="presentation"
    width="48" height="44"} \[Calendar Link\]]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0022}

```{=html}
<aside>
```
::: {.top .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::

::: {.section .feature3}
### AI PRO TIP

About Section Prompt: Help me write a LinkedIn About section using the
PIPA framework (Pain, Insight, Position, Action). My target audience is
\[insert your ideal customer or industry; e.g., "fleet managers in the
waste management industry"\].

-   [**Pain:** Start by identifying a common challenge or pain point
    this audience is dealing with. Describe the pain your prospect and
    their stakeholders are feeling.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0023}
-   [**Insight:** Share two to three unique insights, ideas, or truths
    that demonstrate I understand the problem and can help solve it.
    Describe your nonpromotional insights.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0024}
-   [**Position:** Describe who I am, who I help, how I help them, and
    the outcomes I deliver. Keep it practical and outcome‐focused, not
    fluffy.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0025}
-   [**Action:** End with a clear, low‐pressure call to action that
    tells the reader how to engage with me next (schedule a call, send a
    message, etc.).]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0026}

The About statement can be no more than 2,600 characters, and no emojis
please.

::: {.bottom .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::
:::

```{=html}
</aside>
```
[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_226 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="226"}Your About section isn\'t your pitch---it\'s *your
prospect\'s reason* to engage with you. Use the PIPA framework. Speak
their language. Keep it real and relevant. When you do this right, more
prospects and hot leads will come to you.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-152"}
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0010}

## Step 8: Elevate the Featured Section {#c32.xhtml#head-2-152}

Your Featured section is your scroll‐stopper---a powerful highlight reel
of your professional story. It\'s your own personal sales
[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_227 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="227"}page and lead‐generation magnet baked right into your
LinkedIn profile. Sadly, it is the one section most frequently ignored
or suboptimized by sales professionals.

Take Andrew, for example---a sales rep for a rising tech company. He
used his Featured section to showcase a killer client success story. The
post walked readers through how he helped his customer dramatically
boost brand engagement. It wasn\'t braggy---it was relevant. It told a
story. And it was backed by measurable business outcomes.

Then he pinned it in his Featured section. A few weeks later, a prospect
who\'d been quietly vetting vendors reached out. Why? Because his story
answered the question every buyer is silently asking: *Can this person
actually help me?*

This is the power of the Featured section. It\'s visual social proof,
credibility, and authority all wrapped up in a beautiful package that
you control.

**What you should feature:**

-   [High‐performing posts that drive engagement or
    conversation.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0027}
-   [How‐to articles that educate with insights and your industry
    expertise.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0028}
-   [Customer success stories or testimonials that highlight real
    results and relationships.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0029}
-   [Videos and micro‐demos.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0030}
-   [Lead magnets, including downloadable resources, guides, or
    checklists.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0031}
-   [Webinar or podcast links of appearances or content that builds your
    credibility.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0032}
-   [Scheduling link to make it easy for someone to take the next
    step.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0033}

[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_228 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="228"}

::: section
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0011}

### This Is Your Highlight Reel {#c32.xhtml#head-3-101}

If your headline gets them curious, and your About section draws them
in, the Featured section is your highlight reel, which makes them think,
"This is someone I need to talk to."

It\'s important to refresh this section regularly so that there is
always something new.

### How to add or edit your Featured section

1.  [Go to Your Profile.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0034}
2.  [Scroll to the Featured section. If it\'s not there, click "Add
    profile section," then "Recommended," and choose "Add
    featured."]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0035}
3.  [Click the + icon to add a:]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0036}
    -   [LinkedIn post]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0037}
    -   [Article]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0038}
    -   [External link]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0039}
    -   [Media (PDF, presentation, etc.)]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0040}
4.  [Rearrange items by dragging and dropping to feature your strongest
    content first.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0041}
5.  [Refresh regularly to reflect your most relevant, recent
    work.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0042}
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-153"}
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0012}

## Step 9: Leverage the Services Section {#c32.xhtml#head-2-153}

The Services section on your LinkedIn profile is an underutilized
business development tool available to sales professionals, consultants,
and service providers. This section allows you to use rich media to
articulate value clarity and explain exactly how you help your
clients.[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_229 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="229"}

**Why leveraging the Services section matters**

-   [**Boosts your discoverability:** LinkedIn\'s algorithm favors
    profiles that contain a Services section. When your section includes
    relevant keywords and content, you\'re more likely to surface in
    searches.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0043}
-   [**Qualifies you in:** Prospects aren\'t just reading
    profiles---they\'re qualifying vendors. Clear service descriptions
    and rich media make it easier to trust that you and your company
    know what you\'re doing.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0044}
-   [**Provides instant clarity:** When a buyer hits your profile,
    they\'re asking, "Can this person help me?" Your Services section
    helps them answer that question in the affirmative
    faster.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0045}
-   [**Reinforces your positioning:** When paired with your headline,
    About section, and Featured content, the Services section rounds out
    your message: *I help people like you solve problems like this, and
    here is how I actually do it*.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0046}

### How to set up or update your Services section

1.  [Go to Your LinkedIn profile.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0047}
2.  [Click the "Add Profile Section" button underneath your
    headline.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0048}
3.  [Select "Add Services."]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0049}
4.  [Choose your industry and services from LinkedIn\'s predefined
    list.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0050}
5.  [Write a short, tight description (500‐character max) that focuses
    on: Who you help, how you help, the outcomes you create. Treat this
    as an extension of your About statement.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0051}
6.  [Add rich media (up to eight assets)---videos, client testimonials,
    PDF downloads, event recordings, or media
    mentions.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0052}
7.  [Click "Publish."]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0053}

[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_230 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="230"}Review your Services section often and update to keep
it fresh and relevant.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-154"}
[]{#c32.xhtml#c32-sec-0013}

## Step 10: Check Your Activity Section--Are You Showing Up or Sitting Out? {#c32.xhtml#head-2-154}

Your Activity section is the visible proof that you\'re present,
engaged, and adding value on the LinkedIn platform. It is your thought
leadership and personal branding breadcrumb trail.

Buyers are checking it. Before they respond to your connection request,
before they reply to your message, before they book that meeting---they
scroll straight to this section to get a glimpse of what you are all
about.

And the Activity section doesn\'t lie. It answers one of the most
important questions on your buyer\'s mind: Is this person legit?

### How to audit and improve your Activity section

1.  [Go to your profile.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0054}
2.  [Scroll to your Activity section.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0055}
3.  [Click "See all activity" to review everything you\'ve posted,
    liked, and commented on.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0056}
4.  [Ask yourself:]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0057}
    -   [Would I trust or follow this person?]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0058}
    -   [Does this activity align with my personal
        brand?]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0059}
    -   [Am I showing up as a helpful expert?]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0060}
5.  [If you don\'t like what you see, fix it:]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0061}
    -   [Leave thoughtful comments on two or three industry‐relevant
        posts per day.]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0062}
    -   [Post original content once or twice a week (even short‐form
        works).]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0063}
    -   [Reshare content with context---add your take; don\'t just click
        "Share."]{#c32.xhtml#c32-li-0064}

[]{#c32.xhtml#Page_231 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="231"}If you\'re not showing up, if your last post was from
six months ago, or your only engagement is a handful of likes on company
announcements, your prospects notice.

When a decision‐maker is vetting you, your activity feed becomes a
window into your thinking---and a mirror of your credibility. It\'s
where your personal brand is in motion. It\'s the proof that you think
like they think, speak their language, and understand their world.
:::
:::
:::

[]{#c33.xhtml}

::: {#c33.xhtml#book-content}
::: {#c33.xhtml#sbo-rt-content}
<div>

# []{#c33.xhtml#Page_232 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="232"}[]{#c33.xhtml#c33}[33]{.chapterNumber} [Credentials and Credibility]{.chapterTitle} {#c33.xhtml#c33_1}

</div>

::: {.section aria-label="chapter opening"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0001}

*As stakeholders decide if they should meet with you, give you
information, move to the next step, evaluate your proposal and business
case, compare you to your competitors, and ultimately make the decision
to do business with you, they are---consciously and
subconsciously---assessing the risk that doing so might harm them*.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-155"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0002}

## People Don\'t Like Change {#c33.xhtml#head-2-155}

The number one reason why buyers choose not to move forward with you is
not price, product specs, delivery windows, competitors, or any of the
things salespeople too often blame. It\'s the fear of negative future
consequences.

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_233 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="233"}Your prospects live with an underlying fear that change
will make things worse. In his book *Thinking, Fast and Slow*, Daniel
Kahneman, the father of heuristic psychology, writes:

> *Organisms that placed more urgency on avoiding threats than they did
> maximizing opportunities were more likely to pass on their genes. So,
> over time, the prospect of losses has become a more powerful motivator
> on your behavior than the promise of gains*[^4].

This safety bias causes your buyer\'s brain to be more aware of what
could go wrong than what can go right. They are hyper‐tuned‐into
anything about you that is negative. They gravitate toward safe choices.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-156"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0003}

## Emotional Baggage {#c33.xhtml#head-2-156}

Salespeople, as a rule, are not perceived as safe. You pose a threat.
Your buyers are worried. "What if we make a change and things go wrong?"
They worry that you won\'t live up to your promises, that you\'ll
disrupt their business, that you\'ll manipulate them.

And why shouldn\'t they? The salespeople who came before you failed them
when it mattered most.

Buyers remember negative past experiences more vividly than positive
ones, and bring this emotional baggage into their interactions with you,
thus magnifying every flaw, concern, and incongruence they find about
you.

This is why it is a guarantee that every stakeholder will review your
LinkedIn profile every time. And when they do, they will not be looking
for what is right about you---they will be looking for what is wrong.
:::

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_234 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="234"}

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-157"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0004}

## De‐risk Yourself {#c33.xhtml#head-2-157}

The bottom third of your LinkedIn profile is where you de‐risk yourself
in the minds of prospects and referral partners.

Everything you\'ve done to this point---your headline, your About
section, your Featured content---has been about earning attention. But
sales is a trust game. Always has been.

The bottom third of your profile is where you shift the focus from
interesting and credible to safe, capable, and trustworthy---proof that
you\'ve done this before, people trust you, and others have put their
reputations on the line to endorse you.

You\'re not just telling your story here. You\'re removing doubt and
breaking through cynicism, skepticism, and distrust with compelling
evidence that you are the real deal.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-158"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0005}

## Credibility Sections {#c33.xhtml#head-2-158}

There are five key sections in this part of your profile that work
together to tip the scales in your favor:

-   [**Experience:** This is more than a list of job titles. It\'s a
    credibility narrative. Each role should reinforce your relevance,
    your expertise, and your impact. Prospects want to see that you\'ve
    walked the walk---and that others have trusted you in roles that
    matter.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0001}
-   [**Education:** It\'s not just about degrees. It\'s about shaping
    context. Education signals discipline, commitment, and the lens
    through which you see the world.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0002}
-   [**Licenses and certifications:** These badges tell the buyer,
    "I\'ve been tested. I\'ve earned this." If your industry has
    standards, show others that you\'ve met or exceeded
    them.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0003}
-   [[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_235 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="235"}**Skills:** When aligned with your messaging and
    endorsed by others, skills create micro‐validations that reinforce
    your credibility and improve your ranking in search
    results.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0004}
-   [**Recommendations:** These are gold. In a world full of puffed‐up
    claims, recommendations are social proof. They\'re third‐party
    credibility from people who\'ve worked with you, bought from you, or
    trusted you enough to put it in writing.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0005}

When optimized, these five sections turn you from a question mark into a
safe bet. They become your digital trust signals. And in a world where
attention is short and skepticism is high, trust is the ultimate
competitive advantage.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-159"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0006}

## Step 11: Build Out Your Experience {#c33.xhtml#head-2-159}

Your Experience section is where you de‐risk yourself by demonstrating
that you've helped others like your prospect, and that you know how to
deliver results.

This is the section where you show your work. Forget copy‐pasting your
job description. Nobody cares. Your prospects don\'t want a laundry list
of tasks---they want proof. Proof that you\'ve solved problems like
theirs, understand their industry, and working with you is a safe bet.

In one of our LinkedIn Edge workshops, Regina, a SaaS account executive,
rewrote her Experience section to tell her story rather than list
duties. She told the story of how she helped clients implement solutions
that drove efficiency, cut churn, and generated real ROI.

Two weeks later, a prospect mentioned it during a call, "I saw your
profile before we met. I liked what you did with that client in
logistics. We\'ve got a similar challenge."

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_236 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="236"}That\'s the difference. When your profile is written
from the buyer\'s point of view, you\'re no longer just another rep.
You\'re a professional who *gets them*.

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0007}

### Example Experience Section (the Right Way) {#c33.xhtml#head-3-102}

-   [**Account Executive \| SaaS Sales Specialist \| Helping
    Growth‐Stage Companies Scale with
    Confidence**]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0006}

-   [**Company:**]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0007}

    XSaaSAI Solutions

    January 2021--Present

-   [**Description:**]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0008}

    I help business leaders streamline operational workflows, reduce
    headcount, and grow revenue through tailored technology solutions.
    My approach is consultative---because no two businesses are the
    same.

-   [From the first conversation, I work to understand what\'s holding
    your team back. Then I share insights and strategies based on
    what\'s worked for similar clients---always focused on solving
    *your* specific challenges.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0009}

**Here are a few recent wins:**

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0010}
    -   [Helped a logistics firm increase inbound lead conversion by 40
        percent through optimized sales enablement
        workflows]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0011}
    -   [Guided 20‐plus enterprise clients through implementation,
        reducing churn by 25 percent and accelerating
        time‐to‐value]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0012}
    -   [Delivered over \$1M in net‐new revenue to client portfolios
        through smart tech positioning and ROI‐focused
        conversations]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0013}
    -   [Partnered with marketing to boost engagement by 30 percent,
        resulting in more qualified conversations and shorter sales
        cycles]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0014}
    :::

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_237 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="237"}The results speak for themselves. But more importantly,
so do my clients.

```{=html}
<aside>
```
::: {.top .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::

::: {.section .feature3}
### AI PRO TIP

**Experience Prompt 1:** As a \[job title\] in \[industry\], I want my
LinkedIn Experience section to reflect the way I listen, diagnose
problems, and guide buyers toward solutions. Create a profile entry that
focuses less on selling and more on helping.

**Experience Prompt 2:** Write a LinkedIn Experience entry for my role
as a \[job title\] at \[company name\]. Focus on how I help my
customers. Include who I serve, the problems I solve, the outcomes I
deliver, and specific results. Make it sound like it was written for my
buyers---not for a hiring manager.

**Experience Prompt 3:** Write my LinkedIn Experience section with a
focus on storytelling and relevance to potential clients. Start by
helping me articulate my "why" in the style of Simon Sinek---clarify:

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0015}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0016}
        -   [Why do I do what I do?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0017}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0018}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0019}
        -   [What motivates me to serve this
            market?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0020}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0021}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0022}
        -   [Why did I choose this company or
            role?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0023}
        :::
    :::

Next, create a professional title and SEO‐friendly tagline (format:
**TITLE \| Keywords**) for my profile. Once approved, write my full
Experience section using this structure:

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0024}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0025}
        -   [Open with my "why"---what drives me in this
            work.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0026}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0027}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0028}
        -   [Explain what my company does and why I chose to join
            it.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0029}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0030}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0031}
        -   [Highlight how I help clients, including problems I
            solve.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0032}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0033}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0034}
        -   [Include client‐focused outcomes or proof
            points.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0035}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0036}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0037}
        -   [Share what I love about working in this
            role.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0038}
        :::
    :::

-   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0039}
    -   ::: {#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0040}
        -   [Close with a call to connect or a link to my
            website.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0041}
        :::
    :::

Here\'s what you\'ll need from me to get started:

-   [My current job title and company]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0042}
-   [Who I serve (target audience or ICP)]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0043}
-   [What problems I solve]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0044}
-   [A story or example of client success
    (optional)]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0045}
-   [My company or personal website URL
    (optional)]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0046}
-   [Whether I want the tone to be friendly, formal, or
    conversational]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0047}

::: {.bottom .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::
:::

```{=html}
</aside>
```
:::

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_238 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="238"}

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0009}

### Add Rich Media, Links, and Content That Proves Your Value {#c33.xhtml#head-3-104}

LinkedIn gives you the opportunity to level up your Experience section
with links, images, videos, documents, and presentations add to each
role in your Experience section. This is a powerful way to go beyond
telling and show people why you can be trusted by including:

-   [Customer success stories or case studies]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0048}
-   [Slide decks from webinars or product demos]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0049}
-   [Whitepapers or e‐books you contributed to]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0050}
-   [Links to press features or podcast
    appearances]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0051}
-   [Video testimonials or explainer videos]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0052}

Adding media brings your story to life. It gives your prospects
something to explore---and builds confidence that you\'re the real deal.

### How to Edit Your Experience Section on LinkedIn

1.  [[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_239 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="239"}Go to your profile.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0053}
2.  [Scroll to the "Experience" section.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0054}
3.  [Add or edit a role:]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0055}
    -   [To add a new role: Click the "+" icon in the top‐right corner
        of the Experience section.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0056}
    -   [To edit an existing role: Click the pencil icon next to the job
        you want to update.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0057}
4.  [Update your title, company, and timeframe. Use a positioning
    statement in your job title. For example, instead of just "Account
    Executive" write:]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0058}
    -   [Account Executive \| SaaS Specialist \| Driving Digital
        Transformation for Healthcare Providers]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0059}
5.  [Tell your story in the description field. This is where you make it
    count. Use the Challenge, Solution, Outcome micro‐story
    format:]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0060}
    -   [Identify the challenges your customers
        faced.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0061}
    -   [Explain how you helped them.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0062}
    -   [Articulate the specific outcomes they derived from your
        solution.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0063}
6.  [Add skills to make your experience keyword rich, searchable, and
    easy to scan.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0064}
7.  [Scroll down to the "Media" section at the bottom of the job
    listing.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0065}
8.  [Click "Upload" to add a file (PDF, PPT, JPG, etc.), or "Link" to
    add a URL.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0066}
9.  [Add a title and description to explain what the viewer is
    seeing---and make it count. Use keywords and include your role in
    the project or outcome.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0067}
10. [Save and review your work.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0068}

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_240 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="240"}When you combine powerful messaging with rich content,
your Experience section becomes a proof point that sets you apart.
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-160"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0010}

## Step 12: Optimize Your Education Entries {#c33.xhtml#head-2-160}

Your education section signals credibility, discipline, and your
professional journey---it's far more than just a list of degrees.
Instead of simply listing school names and dates, use this valuable real
estate to differentiate yourself and add dimension to your profile.

Highlight the schools and programs that shaped you, but take it a step
further by adding relevant context. Did you complete a senior project
that relates to the work you do now? Did you lead an organization,
publish research, or win a leadership award? These details help bring
your academic experience to life.

Even better, enhance these entries with rich media. Upload a photo from
a keynote you gave on campus. Link to a research article or capstone
project. Add a video from a business competition or club presentation.
This type of media turns your education from background noise into
visual proof of who you are and what you\'ve accomplished.

You can even include the work you do at your alma mater as an alumni.
For example, Jeb gave the commencement speech at his university.
Education doesn\'t need to sit silently at the bottom of your profile.
Use it to entice connection and reinforce your credibility.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-161"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0011}

## Step 13: Add Licenses and Certifications That Mean Something {#c33.xhtml#head-2-161}

The licenses and certifications section is where you tell the world:
I've done the work, earned the credentials, and I'm qualified.

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_241 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="241"}For sales professionals, this section is often
underutilized or bloated with irrelevant fluff. You don\'t need 50
badges---just the ones that matter. Prioritize certifications that:

-   [Are relevant to your role, industry, or
    solution]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0069}
-   [Signal a level of trust, skill, or
    authority]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0070}
-   [Give prospects confidence in your
    credibility]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0071}
-   [Align with keywords that improve your position in search
    results]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0072}

Include industry‐specific programs, technical credentials, relevant
product certifications, and courses that deepen your value in the eyes
of prospects. If you\'re certified in a selling methodology (like SPIN,
MEDDIC, Challenger, Sales EQ, or Fanatical Prospecting), list it. If
you\'ve completed a course on [Sales Gravy
University](https://www.salesgravy.university/), add it, especially if
it aligns with your current role. Always include:

-   [The issuing organization]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0073}
-   [A brief, compelling description of what the certification
    covers]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0074}
-   [Any relevant links or supporting material]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0075}
-   [When possible, add a link or upload your
    certificate]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0076}

Here again you have the opportunity to add rich media that supports your
credentials. Take advantage of it. That visual stamp of credibility goes
a long way when someone is sizing you up at a glance. It tells
decision‐makers that you\'re a serious professional, invested in your
craft, and continually sharpening your edge.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-162"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0012}

## Step 14: Skills and Endorsements {#c33.xhtml#head-2-162}

The skills section is fuel for LinkedIn\'s search engine. These skills
and keywords are also indexed on Google, Bing, and AI search engines
like ChatGPT, Perplexity, and Gemini.

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_242 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="242"}The right keywords in your skills section can make the
difference between getting found or getting overlooked. LinkedIn lets
you list up to 50 skills. Don\'t waste that real estate. Stack it with
relevant, high‐impact skills that align with what your buyers are
searching for. If you want to show up when your prospect searches for
"fractional CFO," "enterprise SaaS," or "fleet management sales," then
those terms better be listed in your skills.

Be bold and specific here. Pick skills that directly align with how you
solve problems---keywords your buyers would type into search, skills
that match what you discuss in your headline, About section, and
Experience. This consistency creates cohesion and drives higher search
rankings.

-   [**Lead with the right three.** Pin your top three most relevant
    skills at the top of the list. These are ones LinkedIn highlights,
    people see first, and the most likely to get endorsed. They carry
    the most weight, so make them count.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0077}
-   [**Align your skills with your story.** If your About and Experience
    sections talk about solving complex problems, but your skills list
    reads like a software manual, you\'ve got a
    disconnect.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0078}
-   [**Give endorsements to get endorsements.** It\'s a simple gesture,
    but it often sparks a chain reaction. You give a little credibility,
    you get some back. And the more endorsements you rack up on your top
    skills, the more weight they carry with profile viewers. When
    someone sees your top three skills backed by names they know, it
    de‐risks you. It\'s social proof money can\'t
    buy.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0079}

Don\'t phone this in. The Skills section is one of the few places where
the algorithm and the human eye meet. Use it to make yourself findable,
credible, and clear.

```{=html}
<aside>
```
::: {.top .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::

::: {.section .feature3}
### AI PRO TIP

-   **Skill Prompt 1:** I work in the \[industry\] sector and specialize
    in \[solution/role\]. What are the most relevant LinkedIn skills and
    search keywords for someone in my position to get discovered by
    potential clients or employers?
-   **Skill Prompt 2:** I\'m a \[job title\] who sells
    \[product/service\] to \[target audience/industry\]. What are the
    most important hard and soft skills I should include on my LinkedIn
    profile to attract buyers and decision‐makers in this space?

::: {.bottom .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::
:::

```{=html}
</aside>
```
:::

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_243 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="243"}

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-163"}
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0014}

## Step 15: Get Recommendations {#c33.xhtml#head-2-163}

Every great salesperson knows that what others say about you carries
more weight than what you say about yourself.

LinkedIn recommendations are a referral and a five‐star review rolled
into one. In sales, nothing speaks louder than a client or colleague
saying, "This person made a real impact." It\'s the ultimate de‐risker.

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0015}

### The Recommendations You Give and Get Deliver Impact in Unexpected Ways {#c33.xhtml#head-3-105}

Allan, an account executive, had just wrapped a complex rollout with a
major client. It was a tough project---tight timelines, multiple teams,
a hundred moving parts. But one person kept it all glued together: Jen,
the client\'s project manager.

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_244 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="244"}She was a force. Clear communicator. Sharp operator.
Always one step ahead. Because of her leadership, the project didn\'t
just meet expectations---it launched three weeks early. Allan was so
impressed, he wrote her a glowing recommendation on LinkedIn,
spotlighting her strengths and the success they\'d shared.

That single, kind gesture was paid back tenfold. A few months later,
Allan got a message from one of Jen\'s connections. He\'d seen the
recommendation, checked Allan out, and wanted to talk about a project of
his own. Just like that, a deal opened---not from a cold call, but from
a public display of appreciation and proof of performance. That\'s the
magic of LinkedIn recommendations.

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0016}

#### Give to Get {#c33.xhtml#head-4-5}

The best way to get recommendations is to start giving them. Write
sincere, specific, and story‐driven recommendations for people you\'ve
worked with---especially clients and colleagues who brought out your
best work. When you lift others up, two things happen: They\'re more
likely to return the favor, and your name stays visible on their
profiles.

Avoid vague praise. Instead, focus on real results and shared wins. What
did you solve? What changed because of your work? Who benefited? For
example:

> *Jen led a cross‐functional team during our software rollout, keeping
> everyone aligned and on schedule. Her ability to drive collaboration
> helped us launch three weeks ahead of target. I\'d work with her again
> in a heartbeat*.

When you write a thoughtful recommendation for a client, it\'s not just
a kind gesture---it\'s smart positioning. Your words
[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_245 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="245"}show up on their profile, increase your visibility to
their network, reinforce your role as a trusted partner, and make your
client feel very important.
:::

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0017}

#### How to Ask for a Recommendation {#c33.xhtml#head-4-6}

The key to getting recommendations is to ask for them. When asking,
timing is everything. Ask right after a successful project or milestone,
when your impact is fresh in their mind. When asking for a
recommendation:

-   [**Be specific.** Don\'t just ask, "Can you write me a
    recommendation?" Tell them what you\'d like them to focus
    on.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0080}
-   [**Explain why.** Let them know their words will help others
    understand the value you bring---and that their recommendation will
    live on your profile as a testimonial.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0081}
-   [**Make it easy.** Offer to provide a short draft they can tweak.
    This helps them get started and ensures the right story gets
    told.]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0082}

> *\[Name\], I\'ve really enjoyed working with you on \[specific project
> or milestone\]*.
>
> *I\'m writing to ask if you\'ll be open to posting a short LinkedIn
> recommendation about your experience working with me? It will mean a
> lot, and it helps others see the kind of work I do through the lens of
> people I\'ve worked with*.
>
> *If it\'s helpful, I\'m happy to send over a quick draft you can edit
> or use as a starting point if that makes things easier for you*.
>
> *Thanks again---it\'s been a pleasure collaborating with you!*

```{=html}
<aside>
```
::: {.top .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::

::: {.section .feature3}
### AI PRO TIP

To make it easier for your client to post a recommendation, offer to
write a draft for them. Give your AI this prompt:

I need help drafting a LinkedIn recommendation that my client will post
about me and my work. Please interview me by asking the following
questions one at a time:

-   [What specific challenge did this client face before working with
    me?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0083}
-   [What solution did I provide them?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0085}
-   [What stood out to them during our work together---any "aha" moments
    or key mindset shifts?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0086}
-   [What measurable or meaningful outcome did they achieve as a result
    (qualitative or quantitative)?]{#c33.xhtml#c33-li-0087}

Please use my answers to craft a LinkedIn recommendation that highlights
the value I delivered for my client and why other people and business
like them should also choose to work with me.

::: {.bottom .hr}

------------------------------------------------------------------------
:::
:::

```{=html}
</aside>
```
:::

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_246 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="246"}

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0019}

#### Recommendations Are Living Proof That You Are Trustworthy {#c33.xhtml#head-4-7}

Recommendations are more than professional compliments. They\'re solid,
visible evidence that you\'re someone worth trusting. In sales, trust is
currency. Every recommendation on your profile chips away at skepticism
and reinforces that you deliver real value to real people in the real
world.

Don\'t wait for people to recommend you. Earn it. Ask for it. It\'s a
strategic asset that works for you long after the deal is closed.
:::
:::

[]{#c33.xhtml#Page_247 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="247"}

::: section
[]{#c33.xhtml#c33-sec-0020}

### Your LinkedIn Profile Is a Runway to Higher Credibility and More Leads {#c33.xhtml#head-3-106}

You\'ve just rebuilt your LinkedIn profile from the ground up. No more
fluff. No more filler. Every section now works together to tell a
single, powerful story---who you help, how you help, why you\'re worth
talking to, and that you are low risk and trustworthy.

From your banner to your About section, your content to your credibility
signals, you\'ve created a scroll‐stopping, trust‐building,
lead‐generating digital presence that gives you an unfair advantage.

Here's the best part: while your competitors treat LinkedIn like a
digital résumé, you're treating it like a launch pad for visibility,
influence, and opportunity. Your personal brand is no longer an
accident---it's a strategic sales weapon.
:::
:::

:::

[]{#p05.xhtml}

::: {#p05.xhtml#book-content}

::: {.section .part role="doc-part" aria-labelledby="p05_1"}
<div>

# []{#p05.xhtml#Page_249 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="249"}[]{#p05.xhtml#part}[PART 5]{.partNumber} [Differentiation, Thought Leadership, and Authority]{.partTitle} {#p05.xhtml#p05_1}

</div>

::: {.section aria-label="chapter opening"}
[]{#p05.xhtml#p05-sec-0001} []{#p05.xhtml#Page_250 .pagebreak
role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="250"}

> **Be daring, be different, be impractical, be anything that will
> assert integrity of purpose and imaginative vision against the
> play‐it‐safers, the creatures of the commonplace, the slaves of the
> ordinary.**
>
> ***---Cecil Beaton***
:::
:::
:::
:::

[]{#c34.xhtml}

::: {#c34.xhtml#book-content}
::: {#c34.xhtml#sbo-rt-content}
<div>

# []{#c34.xhtml#Page_251 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="251"}[]{#c34.xhtml#c34}[34]{.chapterNumber} [Authority in the Age of Transparency]{.chapterTitle} {#c34.xhtml#c34_1}

</div>

::: {.section aria-label="chapter opening"}
[]{#c34.xhtml#c34-sec-0001}

*We live in the age of transparency. The access to information is
ubiquitous. Anyone with a screen and a few minutes can find practically
anything they\'re looking for. And while that may sound like a good
thing, more often than not it creates confusion for buyers*.

When everything looks the same, when every vendor makes the same
promises, when every blog sounds like it was written by ChatGPT on a
deadline---buyers don\'t get clarity. They get paralysis.

Imagine a stakeholder researching software vendors. What do they find?

-   [Four companies claiming to solve the same
    problems]{#c34.xhtml#c34-li-0001}
-   [Four websites that look nearly identical]{#c34.xhtml#c34-li-0002}
-   [Four blogs with copycat articles]{#c34.xhtml#c34-li-0003}
-   [[]{#c34.xhtml#Page_252 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
    aria-label="252"}Four identical landing pages offering four nearly
    identical white papers]{#c34.xhtml#c34-li-0004}
-   [Four explainer videos full of jargon and
    buzzwords]{#c34.xhtml#c34-li-0005}
-   [An AI‐produced briefing on the pros and cons of each but no
    definitive answers or recommendations]{#c34.xhtml#c34-li-0006}

They contact four account executives only for each account executive to
roll in with a beautifully polished pitch deck, reciting the same
generic messages, touting the same differentiators, and promising the
same future state. Four meetings. Four smiling faces. Four pitches that
all blur together.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-165"}
[]{#c34.xhtml#c34-sec-0002}

## Selling in an Ocean of Sameness {#c34.xhtml#head-2-165}

In this ocean of sameness, differentiating on the attributes of
products, services, software, or price is more difficult than ever
before. Yet far too many salespeople are merely walking, talking
marketing brochures.

Buyers have become cynical and impatient with canned pitches. They want
to engage in conversations with expert professionals who can actually
help them. Therefore, in the age of transparency, the one true way to
differentiate is through your personal brand and authority.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-166"}
[]{#c34.xhtml#c34-sec-0003}

## People Buy You {#c34.xhtml#head-2-166}

People aren\'t buying your product, service, or software. They are
buying you---the person they believe can help them solve their problems.
Strip away the tech. The decks. The features. The marketing brochures.
And what\'s left? One human solving another human\'s problem. That\'s
exactly what sales comes down to at its foundation.

Dr. Robert Cialdini, author of *Influence: The Psychology of
Persuasion*, describes authority as one of the six fundamental
[]{#c34.xhtml#Page_253 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="253"}levers of persuasion. When someone sees you as a
credible expert, they\'re more likely to listen, trust, and follow your
lead.

Studies in behavioral economics show that people are far more likely to
take action when they perceive the source of the recommendation as
credible and competent.

This is the real power of authority in sales. When you\'re seen as a
trusted expert---someone who brings insight, context, and confidence to
the conversation---your recommendations carry weight. Your advice
doesn't feel like a pitch, but rather a path forward.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-167"}
[]{#c34.xhtml#c34-sec-0004}

## Building Authority {#c34.xhtml#head-2-167}

Authority isn\'t just what you know---it\'s how you communicate what you
know. It\'s how you make others feel when they interact with you. Safe.
Seen. Understood.

Building authority is about showing your work, sharing your perspective,
and being visible in the right ways, to the right people, consistently
over time. It\'s about positioning yourself as a trusted advisor worth
listening to.

When you start capturing that insight and sharing it on LinkedIn, you
position yourself as someone worth knowing. In a world flooded with
sameness, authority is the signal that cuts through the noise. It gives
your personal brand power, credibility, and magnetism.

Build your authority. Be the expert. And people will buy you.
:::
:::
:::

[]{#c35.xhtml}

::: {#c35.xhtml#book-content}
::: {#c35.xhtml#sbo-rt-content}
<div>

# []{#c35.xhtml#Page_254 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="254"}[]{#c35.xhtml#c35}[35]{.chapterNumber} [Socially Surrounding Targeted Prospects with the Five S Framework]{.chapterTitle} {#c35.xhtml#c35_1}

</div>

::: {.section aria-label="chapter opening"}
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0001}

*The three primary objectives of slow prospecting are to:*

1.  [Attract prospects to you and generate hot inbound
    leads]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0001}
2.  [Get warm introductions and referrals to target
    prospects]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0002}
3.  [Improve the probability that you get more meetings with less
    resistance from outbound touches]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0003}

Accomplishing these objectives requires surrounding your prospects with
value---consistently showing up in their feeds through original content,
curated insights, and meaningful engagement. This transforms you from
another salesperson pitching into a trusted expert they want to hear
from.

[]{#c35.xhtml#Page_255 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="255"}In the last chapter we discussed why your authority,
presence, and credibility are key differentiators in a world of sameness
and commoditization. Socially surrounding your target audience with
content is how you engineer and operationalize that authority to improve
prospecting outcomes.
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-168"}
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0002}

## The Five S Framework {#c35.xhtml#head-2-168}

The Five S framework is an intentional, strategic formula for
systematically, socially surrounding your prospects so they come to know
you, trust you, and view you as an expert authority in your field. The
framework features:

1.  [Streaming]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0004}
2.  [Sharing]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0005}
3.  [Scrolling]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0006}
4.  [Showing up]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0007}
5.  [Searching]{#c35.xhtml#c35-li-0008}

When executed consistently, this framework becomes a crucial link in
your personal brand building tool kit that helps you build magnetic
authority that attracts new pipeline opportunities and warms up the
coldest of cold calls.

::: section
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0003}

### Streaming: Produce Content Your Prospects Consume {#c35.xhtml#head-3-107}

Streaming is how you insert yourself into your target ICP prospects\'
feed with relevant ideas, stories, and insights that they want to
consume.

When you consistently post original, thought‐provoking content, you
position yourself as someone who understands the space, has a
perspective, and is worth paying attention to.

This is where you start building familiarity at scale. It\'s where your
prospects begin to recognize your voice and face, and associate you with
credibility. On LinkedIn, if you\'re not consistently streaming content,
you become invisible.
:::

[]{#c35.xhtml#Page_256 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="256"}

::: section
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0004}

### Sharing: Earn Reciprocity and Expand Reach {#c35.xhtml#head-3-108}

Sharing is where you become a maven. It positions you as the go‐to
resource for what matters right now in your space. When you share useful
third‐party content, relevant industry articles, or even your
prospect\'s posts, you build goodwill and demonstrate that you\'re tuned
into industry issues and trends.

The beautiful thing about sharing is that you don\'t have to be the
original source. As the curator you get to add your thoughtful comments
and gain authority by association.

As a bonus, when you thoughtfully amplify the voices of others
(especially your prospects), you trigger the law of reciprocity. People
notice. And they remember who elevated them. That's a powerful personal
branding, multi‐threading, and lead generation play.
:::

::: section
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0005}

### Scrolling: Engineer Visibility in Their Feed {#c35.xhtml#head-3-109}

The LinkedIn experience is built around the home screen feed. When you
and your prospects go to LinkedIn, it\'s the first thing you see.

The feed is crucial because it holds users on the platform as they
scroll through it, looking for posts that interest them. What shows up
is driven by LinkedIn\'s mysterious, ever‐changing algorithm. But one
thing we do know about the algorithm is that content engagement drives
visibility in the feed.

Your ultimate goal is to stream and share content that both shows up in
their feed and compels them to *stop their scroll*, click, and engage.
:::

::: section
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0006}

### Searching: Optimize to Get Found {#c35.xhtml#head-3-110}

What you must never forget is that LinkedIn is a massive search engine
that is indexed by other search engines. Optimizing for search is how
you get found.

[]{#c35.xhtml#Page_257 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak"
aria-label="257"}Your headline, profile summary, skills, featured
content, and activity feed are all searchable assets that either help or
hurt your chances of showing up.

Your objective is to be found when buyers go looking for help with the
problems they solve. For this reason you must always be disciplined with
and focused on optimizing your profile and everything you post on
LinkedIn for search.
:::

::: section
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0007}

### Showing Up: Be Present Where It Matters {#c35.xhtml#head-3-111}

Most salespeople lurk. They scroll silently and wonder why nothing
happens.

Showing up is about active participation, not passive scrolling. Move
beyond quick likes to meaningful engagement through thoughtful comments
and genuine conversation. Strategically engage with your prospects,
network, and industry leaders. Turn every comment on your posts into an
opportunity for deeper dialogue.

Showing up gives you an advantage because when you\'re present, visible,
and generous with your attention, people start to feel like they know
you (familiarity). And when they know you, they trust you.
:::
:::

::: {.section aria-labelledby="head-2-169"}
[]{#c35.xhtml#c35-sec-0008}

## A Steady Drumbeat of Credibility {#c35.xhtml#head-2-169}

The Five S\'s are the key to surrounding your prospects with a steady
drumbeat of credibility and staying top‐of‐mind without being in their
face.

This is how you prospect without prospecting, earn authority and become
the safe choice, cross the familiarity threshold, and separate yourself
in a sea of sameness. And the moment they need help solving a problem,
you\'re already there.
:::
:::
:::

[]{#c36.xhtml}

::: {#c36.xhtml#book-content}
::: {#c36.xhtml#sbo-rt-content}
<div>

[]{#c36.xhtml#Page_258 .pagebreak role="doc-pagebreak" aria-label="258"}
